4.10Localisation and language reviewer

Reviewer file: docs/review/localisation.md. Findings are listed in full in Appendix A.

Verdict

Tengo has good plumbing for 11 languages and no translations. All 10 non-English locale files are byte-identical copies of en.json, and status.json marks every one pending. The pipeline that is meant to fill them has a blocking defect: Google Cloud Translation's supported-language list does not include Tshivenda. Because the script processes ve ninth and only writes status.json at the end, a full run would fail partway and leave state that the sync script then overwrites with English. The parts that exist are well thought out. Keys stay in step, the picker labels each language's translation status, <html lang> follows the chosen language, Tshivenda headings switch to a font that has the letters, placeholders are protected, and dates are kept in en-ZA instead of thin CLDR data. What is missing matters more for a money product: no glossary for the three state words, no per-string review status, hard-coded word order in several places, an SMS length check that only works for English, and no first-language review process. As it stands, a judge who picks isiZulu sees English with a notice saying so. If the script is run as written and shipped, people would get machine-translated money advice of uneven quality, with nothing in the message itself saying so.

What works

  • Key parity is enforced. npm run i18n:check passes with "All 11 locales have the same 565 keys" (run on 2026-10-20). scripts/i18n-sync.mjs:33 refreshes pending languages from English and makes changed strings in machine or reviewed languages fall back to English. Note: the fact sheet says 577 keys. The check reports 565.
  • The status is honest and visible where language is chosen. src/components/LanguageSelector.tsx:53-57 shows "Translation on the way. This language shows English for now." for any language that is not English. The note is linked to the select by aria-describedby (line 42). Each option carries its own lang attribute (line 46).
  • <html lang> follows the selection (src/components/Preferences.tsx:12, set before first paint in index.html:18). The Inbox message bubble has its own lang={message.language} (src/pages/Inbox.tsx:117), so the message language can differ from the app language.
  • Tshivenda headings are handled correctly at the CSS level. src/styles/index.css:84-86 switches --font-heading to Inter for html[lang='ve']. My glyph check (docs/review/tools/localisation/glyphs.mjs) confirms Figtree lacks ḓ ḽ ṅ ṋ ṱ and their capitals, and Inter's bundled latin-ext subset has all ten. The pseudo-localisation run confirms the computed h1 font is "Inter Variable" when lang is ve (docs/review/tools/localisation/expansion-flat-home-390.json).
  • The translation script has sensible guards (scripts/translate.mjs:51-66,111-116). {{placeholders}} and brand words are wrapped in translate="no", *bold* becomes <b>, house-rule characters are stripped, and any string whose placeholders change is kept in English. The script runs at build time only, so the app never calls Google.
  • Plural keys use i18next _one and _other (18 pairs in en.json), which i18next resolves through Intl.PluralRules. In Node 26 (ICU 78.3, CLDR 48), all 11 codes are supported and return one/other (docs/review/tools/localisation/plurals.mjs).
  • Keeping dates in en-ZA was a reasonable call. CLDR output for these locales is thin or odd. Node prints zu as "Bil, Okt 27" and xh as "Okt 27, Lwesb", and nr, ss, ve and ts fall back to en-US ("Tue, Oct 27") (plurals.mjs output). Using en-ZA for every language (src/i18n/languages.ts:39) avoids US-ordered dates.
  • Rand formatting is consistent. formatZAR (src/lib/format.ts:6-15) produces "R1 250" with a no-break space and a decimal point. Intl en-ZA would give "R 1 250,50", so the hand-rolled formatter avoids a comma decimal that many readers would not expect.
  • The unconfirmed name origin is stated in the UI rather than hidden (en.json:460: "still to be confirmed by a Tshivenda speaker").

What to improve

L10N-01 Ten of the 11 languages are English, so the 11-language claim is not yet true

Severity: High Evidence: cmp shows every file in src/i18n/locales/*.json is identical to en.json. src/i18n/status.json lists all 10 as pending, and src/i18n/translated-from.json is {}. The fact sheet's "Languages" section agrees. A user who picks isiZulu, Sepedi or Tshivenda gets English plus a notice. Recommendation: Do not present "11 languages" as a feature in the pitch until at least the pilot languages are translated and checked. Say instead "built for 11, with N live". For the hackathon, pick two or three languages that fit the personas' area (Gauteng home languages, Census 2022: isiZulu 23.1 percent, Sesotho 13.1, Sepedi 12.6, Setswana 10.4; Stats SA P0301.4 Table 2.9). Have a first-language speaker write or post-edit at least the messages.*, state.*, hero.*, stateCard.*, sts.* and nav.* keys, and mark them reviewed. Effort: M

L10N-02 Tshivenda is not in Google Cloud Translation, and a full run would fail and leave the files in an inconsistent state

Severity: High Evidence: The Cloud Translation supported-language page (accessed 2026-10-20) lists af and zu under Translation LLM, and xh, st, nso, tn, ts, ss and nr under NMT. It does not mention Venda, Tshivenda or ve. Consumer Google Translate added Venda in June 2024, but the API page used by the script does not list it. scripts/translate.mjs:16 includes ve in ALL, and line 76 throws on any non-OK response. The loop writes each locale file as it finishes (line 123), but status.json and translated-from.json are written only after the loop (lines 127-128). A run that fails at ve (ninth of ten) therefore leaves af to ss translated on disk but still pending in status.json. The next npm run i18n:sync then replaces them with English (scripts/i18n-sync.mjs:33), wasting about 137 000 characters of quota. Not run, because there is no API key. The exact error code is an expectation based on the documentation. Separately, the README's estimate of "about 155,000 characters" for 10 languages is low: en.json has 17 160 characters of strings, or about 171 600 for 10. Recommendation: Remove ve from the machine translation list and have a human translate Tshivenda. Make the script write status.json and translated-from.json after each language, or catch per-language errors and continue. Add a dry-run that checks each target against the API's languages endpoint first. Correct the README figure. Effort: S

L10N-03 Machine-translated money messages would ship without a glossary or context, and with no caveat in the message itself

Severity: High Evidence: scripts/translate.mjs:74 sends bare strings with source: 'en'. It sends no key path or context, and uses no glossary: v2 has no glossary support, and the script uses v2. 156 of 565 strings are 12 characters or fewer, including state.enough, state.tight and state.short ("Enough", "Tight", "Short"). Out of context, "Short" can be read as a length and "Tight" as a fit (Assumption: likely for at least some languages, which only a speaker can confirm). The state word is interpolated into every message: messages.whatsapp.* "Cover: {{state}}" and messages.sms.* "({{state}})" (src/messages/templates.ts:17). The "Machine translated with Google Translate" notice appears only beside the language select (LanguageSelector.tsx:53-57, used in Picker.tsx:65, Me.tsx:82 and Inbox.tsx:135). It does not appear on Home or in the WhatsApp or SMS text that would reach the user. Public evidence on quality is weak. Google said of the 2022 zero-shot languages, including Sepedi and Tsonga, that the method "isn't perfect". It added Venda and Swati in 2024 with PaLM 2 and noted that it is "impossible to pick a 'right' variety". A Stellenbosch study of Afrikaans to English Google output found omission, mistranslation, non-translation and grammar to be the main error types, and quality stopped improving after 2012. News24 (July 2024) reported Tshivenda and siSwati output that diverged from the intended meaning (headline and search summary only, because the page returned 403). Recommendation: Never show machine output for the messages, state, hero, stateCard, sts, plan loan-check and help keys until a first-language reviewer has signed them off. Fall back to English for those keys until then. Build a short term base (Enough, Tight, Short, Safe to spend, Cover, Next money, Plan) per language, written by speakers, and enforce it. Move to Cloud Translation v3 with a glossary if MT stays in the loop. Send each string with its key and an English context note. Keep the machine-translation notice in both the language and English, and show it in the app shell when the status is machine. Effort: M

L10N-04 Review status is per language, so "checked by a first-language speaker" can become untrue

Severity: Medium Evidence: scripts/translate.mjs:124 leaves a reviewed language as reviewed even after it machine-translates new or changed strings into it, and --force re-translates every string while keeping the status. LanguageSelector.tsx:56 then shows language.reviewed: "Translated and checked by a first-language speaker." TranslationStatus in src/i18n/languages.ts:29 has no per-key notion. Recommendation: Track status per key, for example reviewed-from.json that maps each key to the English text and the reviewer. Show "reviewed" only when every user-facing key in the language is reviewed. Refuse --force on reviewed languages unless a flag names the keys. Effort: M

L10N-05 The page declares a language its text is not in

Severity: Medium Evidence: Choosing Tshivenda sets <html lang="ve"> (Preferences.tsx:12), but the content is English because the status is pending. The pseudo-localisation run records lang: "ve" on English pages. The Inbox bubble is marked lang="ve" over English text (Inbox.tsx:117). After a machine run, any string that falls back to English (i18n-sync.mjs:19-20, translate.mjs:113) also sits under the wrong lang. WCAG 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 require the declared language to match the content, and a screen reader may read English with a Tshivenda or default voice. Recommendation: Set <html lang> to the language actually rendered: en while the status is pending. Wrap English fallback strings in lang="en", for example by having a small T component compare the resolved string with English. Effort: S

L10N-06 Word order and agreement are fixed in code and by interpolated fragments

Severity: Medium Evidence: Code joins a fragment and a date in English order: WhyDrawer.tsx:105 and Import.tsx:260 ({t('hero.enoughUntil')} {date}), and privacy/Review.tsx:22 (`${label} ${date} (${state})`). DateHero.tsx:9 shows "Enough until" above the date even for Tight, which conflicts with the "Tight" chip below it (screenshot docs/review/tools/localisation/shots/pseudo-ve-flat-home-360.png). Translated sentences receive a translated state word ({{state}}) and an English date ("Fri 23 Oct", from formatDayShort with en-ZA, src/lib/format.ts:31). In conjunctively written languages (isiZulu, isiXhosa, siSwati, isiNdebele), the word around a date or number usually takes a prefix or concord. Assumption: a translator will want forms such as a locative prefix attached to the date. That cannot be done when the date is a fixed English token, and it can break WhatsApp *bold* if the asterisk lands inside a word. messages.whatsapp.tip.short names a UI label ("Check Plan in Tengo") that MT translates separately from nav.plan. Recommendation: Replace every concatenation with a single key that contains {{date}}, for example hero.enoughUntilDate. Give each state word its own full sentence per state instead of interpolating {{state}}. Add month and weekday names to the locale files so translators control them and the format, while keeping en-ZA as the fallback. Write the "Plan" reference as $t(nav.plan) nesting so it always matches. Ask reviewers to check bold markers on a real WhatsApp client. Effort: M

L10N-07 The SMS length check only works for English and GSM characters

Severity: Medium Evidence: SMS_LIMIT = 160 (src/messages/MessageWriter.ts:28). The Inbox counter uses message.text.length (Inbox.tsx:129), and the only test checks English (src/messages/templates.test.ts:17-21). The GSM 7-bit default alphabet has no ê, ô, ë, š, ḓ, ṱ or ṅ. A message containing any of them is sent as UCS-2, with at most 70 characters per SMS (Wikipedia, GSM 03.38). These letters are needed for Afrikaans (ê, ë, ô), Sepedi (š) and Tshivenda. Today's Short SMS for Ayanda is 101 characters. At 40 percent expansion it would be about 141 characters, which is more than two UCS-2 segments (Assumption: 30 to 50 percent expansion for these languages). Recommendation: Count segments by encoding (GSM-7 or UCS-2), not by characters. Add a test that renders every SMS template in every language with long names and dates and checks segment count. Allow a shorter per-language SMS variant. Bring per-segment cost into the financial model. Effort: S

L10N-08 The layout clips expanded text in the bottom nav, persona rows and segmented controls

Severity: Medium Evidence: I ran a pseudo-localisation test (docs/review/tools/localisation/expansion.l10n.ts) at 360 and 390px with lang="ve", using Tshivenda letters as padding. With a flat 40 percent expansion, the main reading paths wrap without page overflow. With the W3C/IBM size bands (short strings at least double), the bottom tab bar truncates "Money" and "Inbox" on every page (grid-cols-5, text-[12px], truncate, src/layouts/AppLayout.tsx:171,185; screenshot shots/pseudo-ve-ibm-inbox-360.png). On Me, whitespace-nowrap state chips (src/components/StateChip.tsx:18) squeeze persona descriptions to "E-hailing d…" and "Spaza shop o…" (shots/pseudo-ve-flat-me-360.png). The WhatsApp or SMS channel control loses its shape and stacks (shots/pseudo-ve-ibm-inbox-360.png). In the header, the demo chip pushes the privacy icon to the edge, although that chip is kept in English, so real translation would affect it less. The other clipped items in the JSON are screen-reader-only spans and user-entered descriptions. Recommendation: Allow nav labels to wrap to two lines or use shorter per-language labels approved by reviewers. Let state chips shrink or move below the name on narrow screens. Make the segmented control wrap cleanly. Add the pseudo-locale as a permanent Playwright project. Effort: S

L10N-09 The quality gates do not check translation quality

Severity: Medium Evidence: scripts/i18n-check.mjs checks only that keys match and that no string contains an exclamation mark. It does not check placeholder parity across locales (only the translate script does, at the moment of translation), *bold* parity, em or en dashes, or how many strings are identical to English. scripts/fonts-check.mjs passes, but only because every locale file is English. It has never seen a Tshivenda letter in a locale file. Recommendation: Extend i18n:check to compare {{var}} sets and bold-marker counts with English for every key, reject U+2013 and U+2014, and report per language the share of strings still identical to English, so the status cannot say machine or reviewed while 100 percent is English. Add a fixture with real Tshivenda text to the font check. Effort: S

L10N-10 Plural rules need briefing and testing

Severity: Low Evidence: Intl.PluralRules for zu and nso returns one for 0 as well as 1. i18next therefore renders _one for a count of 0 (plurals.mjs: "zu: count=0 -> '0 day'"). Counts can be 0 in privacy.deleteTx, privacy.deleteAudit, money.count and import.skipped. Node 26 supports all 11 codes. Assumption: older Android WebViews may ship trimmed ICU data without nr, ss, ve or ts, in which case the rules resolve to the default locale. This is untested in the app. There are no plural tests for any language other than English. Recommendation: Tell translators that the _one form covers 0 in isiZulu and Sepedi. Add a unit test that renders every plural key in every language for counts 0, 1 and 2. Avoid count-0 sentences where an empty state reads better. Effort: S

L10N-11 Decomposed Tshivenda characters would render in a fallback font

Severity: Low Evidence: The bundled Inter has the precomposed letters but not the combining marks U+032D (circumflex below) or U+0307 (dot above) (glyphs.mjs output: "MISSING" for both fonts). Tshivenda text typed or pasted as base letter plus combining mark, which some keyboards and sources produce, would draw the mark from a system font. translate.mjs does not normalise to NFC. fonts-check.mjs would catch this in locale files, but not in imported CSV descriptions or names. Recommendation: Apply .normalize('NFC') to translated strings, imported descriptions and names. Add NFC as a rule in i18n:check. Effort: S

L10N-12 The name origin is still unconfirmed, and "value" may really be "price"

Severity: Low Evidence: README.md:106-110 and BRIEF.md:11 record "mutengo", Tshivenda for value, as unconfirmed, and the UI says so (en.json:460). My search found no Tshivenda dictionary entry. The only hit lists "mutengo: price" on a Shona vocabulary page (search result summary only, because the page returned 403, so this is unverified). "Price" and "value" carry different meanings for a money brand. Recommendation: Confirm with two Tshivenda speakers, and ideally a lexicographer from a university African languages department, before the pitch. Record the exact gloss and use it consistently in the pitch and the About copy. Have the me.nameBody string in Tshivenda written by a person, not MT. Effort: S

L10N-13 The demo safety label is English in every language

Severity: Low Evidence: translate.mjs:18 keeps common.demoChip as "Demo | Fictional Data" in every language. This is the label that tells users the figures are not theirs. Recommendation: Show it in the chosen language and in English, for example "Demo | Fictional Data" plus a translated line. Effort: S

L10N-14 Every locale is bundled into the main script

Severity: Low Evidence: src/i18n/index.ts:5 imports all locale files eagerly. Per the fact sheet, the JSON accounts for 337 KB of the 696 KB bundle. Translated text with non-ASCII letters will grow slightly. A user downloads ten languages they will not read, on prepaid data. Recommendation: Bundle English, and load the selected language with a dynamic import that the service worker caches for offline use. Effort: S

First-language review process (step by step)

  1. Recruit two reviewers per language, one translator and one checker, both first-language speakers. At least one should live in or near the target users' area (for these personas, Gauteng). Pay them, and record their names and dates against each key.
  2. Brief them before they see any text. Explain who the users are (irregular earners, low data, shared phones), what the product does and does not promise, and that it is not financial advice. Give the house style (plain words, no exclamation marks or dashes) and the tone: calm, direct and never shaming, especially for Short.
  3. Agree a term base first: Enough, Tight, Short, Cover, Safe to spend, Next money, What helps most, Plan, Money, Inbox, Privacy, statement, bill, income. For each term, record the chosen word, the rejected alternatives and why. Test the three state words with three or more lay speakers for the intended meaning (enough money, nearly not enough, not enough).
  4. Translate or post-edit the money paths first: messages.*, state.*, hero.*, stateCard.*, sts.*, next.*, why.*, plan.* (the loan check especially) and help.*. Then consent and privacy. Then everything else.
  5. Check meaning against the numbers. For each persona's real output (Thabo Tight, Lerato Enough, Ayanda Short), have the reviewer read the Home screen and the WhatsApp and SMS messages, then say back what they would do. A wrong answer is a defect, whatever the grammar.
  6. Check grammar around variables: prefixes or concords on {{date}}, {{count}} and {{state}}, the plural forms (0, 1, 2, 10), and *bold* boundaries on a real WhatsApp client.
  7. Check register: formal or informal address, respect forms, and whether "Hi {{name}}" fits. Check that no line sounds like an instruction to borrow, lend or spend, so that "safe to spend" does not become "you may spend".
  8. Check on screen at 360px in both themes, for truncation, wrapping and the Tshivenda glyphs in headings.
  9. Check accessibility by ear. Listen to the main screens with a screen reader set to the language, if a voice exists, or confirm that lang is set correctly.
  10. Sign off per key in a reviewed file. Re-review any key whose English changes. Spot-check 10 percent of keys after each release.
  11. Test with five users per pilot language before any real data is used, using the same comprehension questions as step 5.

Risk ranking by language

Speaker shares are Census 2022 "language spoken most often in the household" (Stats SA P0301.4, Table 2.8). Counts are the share applied to the 62 027 503 total population. They are approximate, because the table excludes infants under 1.

Rank Language Census 2022 share (approx. speakers) Why the risk
1 Tshivenda (ve) 2.5% (about 1.6m) Not offered by the Cloud Translation API the script uses (L10N-02). Added to consumer Translate only in 2024. Special letters (L10N-11). The brand name rests on an unconfirmed Tshivenda word (L10N-12). Few reviewers.
2 isiNdebele (nr) 1.7% (about 1.1m) Smallest written language. Added to Google in 2024. Conjunctive writing makes placeholders and agreement hard (L10N-06). Least public evidence of MT quality.
3 siSwati (ss) 2.8% (about 1.7m) Added in 2024. Conjunctive. News24 reported poor 2024 output.
4 Sepedi (nso) 10.0% (about 6.2m) 2022 zero-shot model ("isn't perfect"). š breaks GSM SMS (L10N-07). 12.6% of Gauteng, so many target users.
5 Xitsonga (ts) 4.7% (about 2.9m) 2022 zero-shot model. 7.0% of Gauteng.
6 isiZulu (zu) 24.4% (about 15.1m) Better MT support (listed under Translation LLM), but errors reach the most people, including 23.1% of Gauteng. Conjunctive. 0 takes the singular plural form (L10N-10).
7 Sesotho (st), Setswana (tn), isiXhosa (xh) 7.8%, 8.3%, 16.3% Longer-supported NMT languages. Still need human review of the money terms.
8 Afrikaans (af) 10.6% (about 6.6m) Strongest MT support and many reviewers. ê, ë and ô still force UCS-2 SMS.

Top three risks

  1. A machine-translated state word or loan check gives the wrong meaning in a money decision. For example, Short read as fine, or "safe to lend" read as an instruction, sent by WhatsApp or SMS with no caveat (L10N-03, L10N-06).
  2. The 11-language claim is challenged at judging. Every language shows English, and the chosen MT tool cannot produce one of the 11 languages, which is also the language of the brand's origin (L10N-01, L10N-02, L10N-12).
  3. Trust labels drift from reality. "Checked by a first-language speaker" stays on after unchecked machine strings arrive, and lang declares a language the text is not in (L10N-04, L10N-05).

Score

4/10. The infrastructure is above average for a hackathon build, but no language is translated, the pipeline cannot produce Tshivenda, and there is no review process for money messages.

4.11Product strategist and commercial lead

Reviewer file: docs/review/commercial.md. Findings are listed in full in Appendix A.

Verdict

Tengo asks a sharp question that South African bank apps do not answer today: not "what is my balance" but "until when does it last, given how I actually get paid". I found no verified South African bank or app feature that shows a run-out date against an irregular income cadence. The nearest are FNB nav» Money's "available funds after known upcoming payments" and Standard Bank's "Future Payments" 14 to 30 day estimate. That gap is real, but it is narrow and cheap for a bank to close. The engine is a 368 line file of transparent percentile maths (src/engine/forecast.ts), it has no proprietary data, and every bank already holds the transaction history it needs. The only business model that fits is a B2B2C licence embedded in a bank app, paid as a pilot fee and then a per-active-user fee with a platform floor, with sponsor funding as a secondary track for users outside the partner bank. On my sourced inputs (docs/review/model-inputs.md), one bank at base adoption gives about 44 000 monthly active users. At R5 per user that does not cover a lean team and compliance. Daily paid WhatsApp or SMS messages would cost more per user than the bank is likely to pay. The consumer subscription model does not fit this segment. The pitch is strong as a feature concept for FNB and weak as a standalone company today.

What works

  • A question no verified competitor answers. Home leads with a date, a range and a state (Enough, Tight, Short), with the next money date estimated from weekday or interval patterns (src/engine/forecast.ts, fact sheet "Forecast engine"). Of the competitor features I could verify, FNB nav» Money shows funds left after scheduled payments, with no income projection (BusinessTech, 2018). Standard Bank's Future Payments estimates upcoming payments and spending for 14 to 30 days (Standard Bank). Nedbank MoneyTracker and Discovery's Financial Analyser are budget and category tools with no stated balance forecast (Nedbank, Discovery). The date-first framing for irregular earners is a real point of difference.
  • The segment is large and under-served on resilience. Informal sector employment is 3.6 million and private household employment 1.1 million in Q2 2026 (Stats SA QLFS Q2 2026 presentation, slide 18). Only 30.8 percent of South African adults say it is not difficult to raise extra money within 30 days, and 18.3 percent in the poorest 40 percent (World Bank Little Data Book on Financial Inclusion 2025, South Africa). Account ownership is 81.1 percent, so most of the segment is reachable through a bank.
  • Built to be embedded. All data access goes through TengoRepository (src/data/repository.ts:75), and messages go through a MessageWriter interface (src/messages/templates.ts). That is the right shape for a bank licence, where the bank supplies data and channel.
  • Cheap to run, apart from messaging. The forecast is local, deterministic statistics with no model inference. Messages are fixed templates today, and even an LLM rewrite would cost about R0.002 per message on Claude Haiku 5.5 at USD 0.10 and 0.50 per million tokens (Claude pricing) with my assumed 600 input and 120 output tokens.
  • Culturally specific features. The loan check ("safe to lend up to") answers a common real question about lending to family. The 11 language plumbing exists (npm run i18n:check, fact sheet "Languages"). No verified bank feature offers either.
  • A privacy posture that fits bank procurement. Consent, export, deletion with receipt, an audit log and data minimisation all work against the local store and are clearly labelled as simulated (fact sheet "What is simulated"). This shortens the conversation with a bank's privacy team, even though none of it is production grade.

What to improve

COMM-01. No defensible moat against the bank building it - Severity: High - Evidence: The engine is transparent percentile maths over date, amount and description (src/engine/forecast.ts, src/engine/classify.ts:16). It uses no proprietary data. Banks already ship adjacent features: FNB nav» Money "My available funds" since 2018 (BusinessTech), Standard Bank Future Payments (Standard Bank), Discovery's AI budget tool (Discovery). FNB reports 8.16 million digitally active customers and its own large digital build (BusinessDay, 2026-09-16). - Recommendation: Present the defensible assets honestly: (1) backtested forecast accuracy on irregular income, which no competitor publishes; (2) a tested, plain-language, multilingual content set for money states; (3) a pilot playbook with outcome measurement. Offer the engine under licence with the bank owning the deployment, so "build versus buy" becomes "buy and adapt now". Do not claim a technology moat. - Effort: M

COMM-02. Single-bank unit economics do not break even - Severity: High - Evidence: Base funnel in model-inputs.md section 2: 8.16m digitally active x 15 percent irregular x 60 percent shown x 15 percent activate x 40 percent stay active = about 44 000 MAU (range 6 000 to 220 000). At R5 per MAU (Assumption, R2 to R12) revenue is about R220 000 a month. Variable cost with in-app push is about R1.23 per MAU (hosting R0.15, compute R0.02, LLM R0.06, support R1.00), so contribution is about R166 000 a month against assumed fixed costs of R310 000 (team R250 000, compliance R60 000). Break-even needs about 82 000 MAU at R5. No public per-user prices exist for bank money-insight vendors to anchor a higher fee, and Capitec's entire main account costs R7.50 a month (Capitec 2026 fees). - Recommendation: Price as pilot fee (R150 000 to R1m) plus per-MAU fee plus an annual platform floor (R0.6m to R3m), and plan for a second bank or a white-label channel within 18 months. Model break-even per scenario in model.xlsx with the floor switched on and off. - Effort: M

COMM-03. Daily paid messaging costs more than the licence fee - Severity: High - Evidence: The brief and Inbox describe a daily WhatsApp or SMS message (src/messages/templates.ts, fact sheet "Screens"). A WhatsApp utility template in South Africa is USD 0.0095 per delivered message from 1 October 2026 (ChatMaxima, quoting Meta list rate; Meta confirms a South African utility increase on that date at Meta pricing docs). 30 messages x USD 0.0095 x R16.13 (valutafx) = R4.60 per user per month before BSP fees. If Meta classed the nudges as marketing (USD 0.0379), it would be R18.34. Daily SMS at R0.24 (Panacea Mobile) is R7.20. Each exceeds the base licence fee of R5. - Recommendation: Make in-app push inside the bank app the default channel (no per-message cost). Send WhatsApp only on state changes and at most weekly, and design replies that open the free 24 hour service window. Keep templates rather than an LLM for routine messages. Put message frequency in the model as a top tornado driver. - Effort: S

COMM-04. The segment's cash income is invisible to the product - Severity: High - Evidence: Two of three personas earn cash: Thabo "Cash fares most days", Ayanda "Cash takings come in lumps" (src/i18n/locales/en.json, personas). The repository has no way to add a transaction, only replace history or edit one (src/data/repository.ts:75 to 96). A what-if is temporary. The seed treats cash as bank deposits (src/data/seed/generate.ts:52), which real cash earners often do not make. 19.6 percent of adults save through clubs or people outside the family (Little Data Book 2025), which Tengo also does not model. A bank-data-only forecast will read cash earners as Short when they are not, which damages trust and the commercial case. - Recommendation: Add a quick "cash in" and "cash out" entry and a "cash on hand" figure that feeds the forecast, plus a stokvel contribution as a recurring bill type. Pitch this as the reason Tengo is different from bank budgeting tools. - Effort: M

COMM-05. No evidence of outcomes for a bank buyer - Severity: Medium - Evidence: All data is synthetic and fictional (fact sheet "Data"). There are no users, no test sessions and no measured behaviour change. Every bank value input in model-inputs.md section 5 beyond published fees is an Assumption (unpaid reduction 0 to 30 percent, deposit uplift R0 to R1 000). - Recommendation: Before pitching a pilot, run 5 to 8 user sessions with real irregular earners, and publish the backtest. Offer a pilot design with a control group and four pre-agreed KPIs: activation, 30 day retention, unpaid debit orders per user, and a confidence survey item. - Effort: M

COMM-06. A consumer subscription does not fit the segment - Severity: Medium - Evidence: The target pays R7.50 a month for a whole Capitec account (Capitec 2026 fees). Free budgeting tools exist inside FNB, Standard Bank, Nedbank and Discovery apps (sources above), and Vault22, the platform behind 22seven, offers a free tier (Vault22). Card collection costs 3.2 percent plus R2 (PayFast fees, via search index), about 10 percent of a R29 subscription. Public finance app day 30 retention figures are 4.2 to 15 percent (Panto, weak source). At 2 percent conversion, revenue is about R0.44 per MAU against R1.23 cost and an assumed R120 acquisition cost. - Recommendation: Keep Tengo free to the user. Drop the consumer subscription from the pitch except as a later premium for small business owners such as spaza shops (stock planning), tested separately. - Effort: S

COMM-07. The distribution story conflicts with the product as shown - Severity: Medium - Evidence: The landing page counts "Bank logins needed" as a selling point (src/i18n/locales/en.json landing.stats.logins) and onboarding depends on a CSV statement export (src/pages/Import.tsx). Few informal earners will export a CSV, so this does not scale. Open banking in South Africa is still being designed: trackers give expected dates, but I found no SARB or IFWG document confirming a 2026 start (Open Banking Tracker, weak). The realistic channel is inside the bank app, using the bank's own data. - Recommendation: Tell one distribution story: "inside your bank app, using data the bank already has, with consent". Keep CSV as the demo path and say so. Treat open finance as later upside, not the plan. - Effort: S

COMM-08. The pitch overclaims languages - Severity: Medium - Evidence: The landing page shows an "Official languages" stat (src/i18n/locales/en.json landing.stats.languages). All 10 non-English locales contain English text (fact sheet "Languages", src/i18n/status.json). A judge who picks isiZulu sees English (docs/review/prep/walkthrough.md item 5). - Recommendation: Before submission, show one or two human-reviewed languages and change the stat to "11 languages ready for translation", or remove it. - Effort: S

COMM-09. One buyer, one channel - Severity: Low - Evidence: The product is built for an FNB competition (README line 9). There is no second channel in the code or README. Comparable digital bases exist at Capitec (15.3m active app clients, Capitec FY2026 summary), Absa (5.4m active digital, BusinessDay) and Nedbank (3.2m Money app active, TechFinancials). - Recommendation: Keep FNB as the lead, and prepare a white-label variant and a platform-partner track (for example e-hailing driver programmes; Bolt reports about 500 000 drivers onboarded since 2016, ITWeb). - Effort: M

COMM-10. Brand assets are not commercially cleared - Severity: Low - Evidence: The README notes that the Public Stunt font is free for personal use only and needs a commercial licence, and the name origin is still to be confirmed by a Venda speaker (BRIEF.md sections 1 and 5). The fact sheet lists the logo and icons as placeholders. - Recommendation: Clear the font licence, confirm the name and run a trademark search before any pilot or public launch. - Effort: S

COMM-11. No written business case in the repository - Severity: Low - Evidence: The README and BRIEF cover product and build only, with no section on the buyer, pricing or metrics (README.md, BRIEF.md). - Recommendation: Add a one-page business case: buyer, model (a) with pricing, pilot KPIs, unit cost per MAU and break-even, linked to model.xlsx. - Effort: S

Business model comparison
Model Fit Why
(a) B2B2C licence, per active user, embedded in a bank app Best The bank has the data, the trust, the channel (free push) and the cost of the problem (unpaid items, arrears, contact centre). No CAC for Tengo. Weakness: small per-user fee, long procurement, build versus buy (COMM-01, COMM-02).
(b) Revenue share on bank outcomes Later add-on Aligns incentives, but attribution needs a control group and agreed baselines. Unpaid fees are small per user (R6 at Capitec, R25 pay as you transact and R130 in a bundle after 3 free at Standard Bank, Standard Bank Access 2026), so the share alone will not fund the company. Use as a bonus on top of (a).
(c) Freemium consumer subscription Poor Price sensitive segment, free alternatives, payment fees about 10 percent, weak retention, high CAC (COMM-06).
(d) Sponsored or partner funded for inclusion Secondary Fits users outside the partner bank (cash earners, platform workers). Grants found are small (USD 5 000 per startup in the 2023 SAIB fintech challenge, Engineering News). Useful for research and impact evidence, not as the core revenue.
Pricing
  • Bank: pilot fee R150 000 to R1m for 6 months; then R2 to R12 per MAU per month (base R5) with an annual platform floor of R0.6m to R3m. All Assumption, reasoned in model-inputs.md section 4.
  • Outcome bonus: 10 to 30 percent of measured, control-group-verified benefit (Assumption).
  • Consumer: free. No paid tier until a small business variant is tested.
  • Sponsor: R20 to R150 per reached user per year to cover messaging and support (Assumption).
Channel strategy and partners
  1. Bank app embed (lead): in-app card on the home screen for customers whose deposits look irregular, push notifications, the bank's own consent flow. FNB is the natural first buyer because of the competition; FNB also reports R5.7bn in stokvel deposits, which shows it already serves informal money practices (BusinessDay).
  2. WhatsApp as a light secondary channel, on state change only (COMM-03).
  3. Platform partners: e-hailing and delivery platforms (payout data is regular and digital), and card acceptance providers for small traders such as Yoco, whose settlement data shows trader takings (Yoco fees). No partnership exists; these are targets.
  4. Earned wage access providers such as Paymenow, which serves nearly 500 000 users per BusinessDay 2025 (from search snippet), as a possible partner: a "until when" forecast could reduce over-drawing.
  5. NGOs and inclusion funders for cash earners outside banks (model d).
Competitive landscape (South Africa)

All accessed 2026-10-20. "Not verified" means I could not confirm the feature from a primary or reliable source.

Competitor Type Relevant features Forecasts run-out date for irregular income? Source
FNB app (nav» Money) Bank Smart budget with auto-sorted spend and alerts at halfway, near limit and at limit (2020); "My available funds" after known upcoming payments and debit orders (2018). Current 2026 menu not verified. No (scheduled payments only, no income projection) Sunday World 2020, BusinessTech 2018
Capitec Bank Automatic spend categorisation, money in and out notifications, up to 10 savings accounts. Official page blocked by a bot check; seen only through search snippets. Budget limits not verified. Not verified Capitec blog (snippet only)
Absa Bank Savings Coach: goals, automatic "auto savers", tips, up to 7.75 percent interest. No budgeting or forecasting tool found for the South African app. No evidence found Absa Savings Coach
Standard Bank Bank Budget Manager (pre-populated budget from past spend, goals, category limits); Future Payments estimates upcoming payments and expected spend for the next 14 to 30 days; Money Movements; Money Reels recap (Dec 2025). Partly: near-term spend estimate, no stated run-out date or income cadence Standard Bank app guide, BusinessTech Money Reels
Nedbank Bank MoneyTracker: free, category budgets, up to 8 dashboards incl. side hustle, invoicing. No (page does not mention forecasting) Nedbank MoneyTracker
TymeBank (GoTyme Bank) Digital bank GoalSave up to 10 goals, up to 10 percent interest with conditions; about 12 million customers (date unclear); old app retired 31 Aug 2026 (search snippet). No evidence found GoTyme GoalSave, Daily Investor
Discovery Bank Bank Vitality Money Financial Analyser: 160+ categories, automated or manual budgets, smart alerts, points for budget goals (2023); AI spending insights on WhatsApp (2025). About 1.6m clients. No (article does not mention forecasting) Discovery, News24 2025
Vault22 (22seven) Budgeting app 22seven.com now redirects to Vault22: account linking, budgeting, free, Basic and Premium tiers (prices not shown); shareholders include Old Mutual and SC Ventures. No evidence found Vault22
Paymenow Earned wage access (formal employees) Salary advances; nearly 500 000 users (2025 report). Not built for informal earners. No BusinessDay 2025 (snippet), tech.africa
Yoco Card acceptance for small traders Card machines and online payments; in-person debit rates from 2.30 percent up to R50k turnover (Core). Money-management features for traders not verified. Not verified Yoco fees
MTN MoMo Wallet Relaunched in SA in 2020; card top-ups added with Mastercard (Oct 2025). Current SA user count not verified. No evidence found ITWeb 2020, Engineering News 2025
Vodacom VodaPay Wallet and super app Wallet with Bidvest Bank; 1.6m registered users in May 2022. Current users not verified. No evidence found Telecom Review Africa, Connecting Africa
Personetics, Meniga (B2B vendors) Bank insight engines Entered SA through local partners (2014, 2016). Current SA bank clients not verified. These are the real "buy" alternatives a bank will compare Tengo with. Not verified for SA Personetics 2014, Meniga 2016

Other informal-income tools (for example Kazang, spaza ordering apps) were not checked and are not claimed.

Differentiation and defensibility
  • Differentiation: a date, not a balance; irregular income cadence (weekday or interval); three plain states; safe to spend and safe to lend; multilingual messaging. Verified competitors offer budgets, categories and near-term payment views instead.
  • Defensibility: low. The method is published in the README and is easy to copy. What can become defensible is accuracy evidence from real pilots, tested copy in 11 languages, and a cash-entry habit loop (COMM-04) that bank tools ignore.
Go-to-market
  1. Now to submission: fix COMM-07 and COMM-08 copy, add cash entry if time allows, and pitch model (a).
  2. Next 3 months: 5 to 8 user tests with drivers, hairdressers and spaza owners; publish backtest; one-page business case (COMM-11).
  3. Months 3 to 9: one bank pilot with 5 000 to 20 000 invited customers, push only, control group, four KPIs.
  4. Months 9 to 24: commercial licence with platform floor; second bank or white-label; sponsor-funded track for cash earners.
Strongest and weakest parts of the pitch
  • Strongest: "A balance tells you what you have. Tengo tells you until when." It names a gap no verified competitor fills, for a large, low-resilience segment, in a form that fits inside a bank app.
  • Weakest: there is no reason a bank cannot build it, the per-user economics at one bank are thin, the product ignores cash, and there is no user evidence yet.

Top three risks

  1. Build instead of buy. A bank with FNB's digital scale can reproduce the engine quickly. Tengo is then a hackathon idea, not a supplier (COMM-01).
  2. Thin economics. A low per-user fee, a long procurement cycle and messaging costs can leave the company loss-making even after a successful pilot (COMM-02, COMM-03).
  3. Trust failure with cash earners. Forecasts that ignore cash will say Short to people who are fine, and they will stop using it. Activation and retention will then miss the pilot KPIs (COMM-04, COMM-05).

Score

4 out of 10: a clear, differentiated feature concept with the right embed architecture, but no moat, no user evidence, a blind spot for cash income, and unit economics that only work with a platform floor or more than one bank.

4.12Investor and sceptic

Reviewer file: docs/review/investor.md. Findings are listed in full in Appendix A.

Verdict

Tengo is a well built demo of a sensible idea, but nothing in it would yet convince me to fund it. The product has no users, no real data, no data access path beyond a manual CSV, no buyer, and an engine of about 370 lines of percentile arithmetic (src/engine/forecast.ts) that any bank with transaction data could rebuild in weeks. The one thing it claims as its edge, a date instead of a balance, is a framing, not an asset: FNB has shown "what's left after scheduled payments and debit orders" since 2018, and several consumer apps already sell "safe to spend until payday". Worse, the demo contradicts itself on the screen that matters most (Ayanda is "Short" while being told R310 is "safe to spend today" and the chart below says "Enough"), and the landing page advertises 11 languages that are all English. The honest pitch today is "a credible prototype of a feature a bank could adopt", not "a company". That is fine for a hackathon. It is a no for an investment until there is evidence that real irregular earners understand, trust and act on the date.

What works

  • The question is sharp and easy to say. "A balance tells you what you have. Tengo tells you until when." Evidence: docs/review/screens/landing-light-1280.png, BRIEF.md section 1.
  • The engine is transparent and explainable, which is a real asset for a bank pitch. Every number has a simple rule a compliance team can read: median and percentiles of rolling 7 day spend (src/engine/forecast.ts:59-88), recurring bill rule (src/engine/classify.ts:16-22), state rule (src/engine/forecast.ts:217-221). There is no black box to defend.
  • Engine quality is high for a prototype: 97.6 percent statement coverage in src/engine, 66 unit tests passing. Evidence: docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md (Quality signals).
  • Help is computed with the same engine as the date, so suggestions are internally consistent with the headline. Evidence: src/engine/help.ts:45-76.
  • Privacy posture is a selling point for a bank: no third party requests, data stays in the browser, and simulated controls are labelled as such. Evidence: docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md (What is simulated), BRIEF.md section 2.
  • Front end performance and accessibility scores are strong: Lighthouse mobile landing 94 performance and 100 accessibility. Evidence: docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md, docs/review/prep/lighthouse/.

What to improve

INV-01. Zero evidence of demand or behaviour change - Severity: High - Evidence: No users, no interviews, no test sessions recorded anywhere in the repo. The personas are fictional composites (README.md:11, src/data/seed/personas.ts). The whole thesis, that a date changes behaviour more than a balance, is untested. - Recommendation: Run 5 to 8 sessions with real e-hailing drivers, hairdressers or spaza owners before submission. Show the Home screen, ask "what does this tell you", "what would you do today", and record whether they read the date correctly. Quote them in the pitch. - Effort: M

INV-02. The core insight is easy to copy and partly exists already - Severity: High - Evidence: The forecast is 368 lines (src/engine/forecast.ts) using median and 20th and 80th percentiles. FNB's nav Money "My available funds" has shown what remains after scheduled payments and debit orders since 2018 (BusinessTech, 11 June 2018). Standalone apps already market "safe to spend before your next paycheck" and day by day balance to payday (Product Hunt listing for Pocket Runway; 11:FS payday article). - Recommendation: Stop pitching the algorithm as the moat. Pitch the irregular income specific parts (income rhythm detection without a payday, range band, Short and Tight states, loan to family check) and the design and language work as the asset, and position Tengo as a feature a bank licenses or acquires, not a standalone company. - Effort: S

INV-03. Headline, safe to spend and chart contradict each other - Severity: High - Evidence: Ayanda: hero "Short from Fri 23 Oct", stats card "Safe to spend today R310 until Mon 26 Oct", chart readout for today "Enough" (docs/review/screens/ayanda-home-dark-390.png; persona-snapshot.json). Her usual daily spend is R623. Safe to spend divides the balance over days to the latest next money (src/engine/forecast.ts:291-296) while the date uses the spend rate (forecast.ts:282-288) and the chart adds expected income (forecast.ts:234-267). Also, the label "Enough until" is shown for the Tight state (src/components/forecast/DateHero.tsx:9), visible on the landing hero: "Enough until Tue 27 Oct" next to a "Tight" chip (landing-light-1280.png). - Recommendation: One source of truth per screen. When Short, replace safe to spend with "Spend at most R X a day to reach Sun 25 Oct" derived from the same path, label Tight as "Tight until", and make the chart's today state match the hero or remove it. - Effort: S

INV-04. No path to real data - Severity: High - Evidence: No bank connection, no open banking, CSV upload only, PDF import "coming next" and not built (docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md, Data). South Africa's open finance framework is still being worked on by the IFWG and FSCA, with formal adoption not yet confirmed (Moonstone, 2026; Stitch, 10 July 2026). - Recommendation: Name the data route explicitly: embedded in a bank app using the bank's own transaction feed is the only route that works this year. Show a mock integration contract (inputs Tengo needs: balance, 60 days of transactions, scheduled debit orders). - Effort: M

INV-05. Overclaiming languages - Severity: High (credibility) - Evidence: Landing stat "11 Official languages" (landing-light-1280.png). All 10 non-English locales contain English text (src/i18n/status.json, npm run i18n:check in docs/review/prep/checks.md). The About copy says translations are "machine made or on the way" (src/i18n/locales/en.json:475) when none has been machine translated. - Recommendation: Change the stat to "English now, 10 languages planned" or ship and get first-language review for at least isiZulu and Sepedi or Setswana before submission. A judge who taps isiZulu and sees English will discount everything else. - Effort: S (copy) to M (two reviewed languages)

INV-06. Accuracy is unproven and the demo is tuned to look right - Severity: High - Evidence: The seed balance is chosen by the engine so each persona starts in Tight, Enough and Short (docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md, Data; src/data/seed/generate.ts). The synthetic history was generated by the same team that wrote the detector, so a correct forecast on it proves internal consistency, not accuracy. - Recommendation: Backtest on at least a handful of real, consented, anonymised statements from irregular earners and publish error in days and band coverage. Until then, say "accuracy not yet measured on real data" in the pitch. - Effort: M

INV-07. Who pays is undefined - Severity: Medium - Evidence: No pricing, no business model in README.md or BRIEF.md. The intended users are, by definition, short of money, which rules out a meaningful consumer subscription. Assumption: the only realistic payer is a bank or a funder, at a per active user fee in the low single rands per month (plausible range R1 to R10), because banks already give budgeting tools away free (nav Money is described as free, Engineering News, 28 March 2022). - Recommendation: Pick one model (B2B2C licence to a bank) and state the bank's benefit as a hypothesis to test: fewer failed debit orders and unpaid fees among irregular earners. - Effort: S

INV-08. The headline date ignores income, so it means different things to different people - Severity: Medium - Evidence: Runway runs the balance down with no future income (src/engine/forecast.ts:195-211). Lerato is "Enough until 1 Nov" because rent of R2 800 lands on 1 Nov (persona-snapshot.json), even though she is paid on 24 Oct and again before 1 Nov. For a weekly earner like Thabo the date is almost always about a week out, so it adds little over "next payout". - Recommendation: Test with users which question they think the date answers. Consider phrasing as "Your money now lasts until" and making the state, not the date, the primary signal when next money comes first. - Effort: S

INV-09. Advice-like outputs raise regulatory and harm exposure - Severity: Medium - Evidence: "Safe to spend", "safe to lend" (src/engine/forecast.ts:357-368) and a suggestion to move Thabo's car instalment (persona-snapshot.json, help.billToMove; src/engine/help.ts:58-73). Moving a vehicle finance payment can trigger penalties or arrears. Assumption: a bank's legal team would require disclaimers and possibly reframing before a pilot; see the legal reviewer for the regulatory analysis. - Recommendation: Exclude credit agreement instalments from "bill to move", rename "safe to lend" to "you could lend up to and stay Tight", and add a one line "estimate, not advice" under every figure. - Effort: S

INV-10. Cash and informal money flows are invisible - Severity: Medium - Evidence: The engine sees only bank transactions (src/engine/forecast.ts:105-191). Ayanda's income is "Cash deposit, shop takings" and card machine sales (ayanda-home-dark-390.png). Assumption: a spaza owner pays suppliers and family partly in cash before banking, so the bank balance understates both income and spend; the effect size is unknown and could be large. - Recommendation: Add a manual "cash in hand" and "money I owe or am owed" entry, and test with a cash heavy user. - Effort: M

INV-11. Placeholder brand and unconfirmed name - Severity: Low - Evidence: Logo and icons are placeholders, and the name's Tshivenda meaning is "to be confirmed by a Venda speaker" (BRIEF.md section 1; docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md). - Recommendation: Confirm the name with a first-language speaker and make a simple wordmark before submission. - Effort: S

INV-12. Dependency vulnerabilities and one large bundle - Severity: Low - Evidence: npm audit shows 2 critical and 5 high (mostly dev), 2 moderate in production (react-router); one 696 KB JavaScript file including all locales (docs/review/prep/fact-sheet.md, docs/review/prep/audit.md). - Recommendation: Update react-router and split locale files so a judge's due diligence checklist comes back clean. - Effort: S

Top three risks

  1. The bank builds it. The feature is a few hundred lines on top of data the bank already owns, and FNB has had a forward looking "available funds" view since 2018. If the idea is good, the cheapest path for a bank is to copy the framing, not to buy Tengo (INV-02).
  2. Users do not trust or understand the date. No one has used it. On the demo's own Short persona, three numbers on one screen disagree. A wrong "safe to spend" for someone already short is the exact harm the product claims to prevent (INV-01, INV-03, INV-06).
  3. No data, no product. Without a bank feed the product depends on users exporting CSV statements, which this segment is unlikely to do repeatedly. Assumption: repeat manual import rates below 10 percent of users per month (INV-04).

Ten hardest questions

  1. Question: How many real irregular earners have used this, and what did they do differently? Best honest answer today: None. It is a demo with fictional data. The design is based on three composite personas and published research on income volatility, not on sessions with users. What would strengthen it: Five to eight recorded sessions with drivers, hairdressers or spaza owners, with quotes and a simple measure such as "read the date correctly" and "said they would change a spend today".

  2. Question: How accurate is the date? Best honest answer today: We do not know on real data. On synthetic data the engine is consistent with its own rules, and it shows a range band and a confidence level instead of pretending to be exact. What would strengthen it: A backtest on real, consented statements reporting mean error in days, how often the actual run-out date falls inside the band, and how often the state was right in hindsight.

  3. Question: FNB already shows money left after scheduled debits, and budgeting apps show "safe to spend until payday". What is new? Best honest answer today: The framing and the segment. Those tools assume a payday; Tengo infers next money from irregular deposits (weekday rhythm or gap percentiles), shows a range, and turns it into Enough, Tight or Short. The arithmetic is not new. What would strengthen it: Evidence that irregular earners misread existing tools and read Tengo correctly, from a side by side test.

  4. Question: Why would a bank buy this instead of building it in a sprint? Best honest answer today: It probably would build it. Tengo's value is a tested design, copy and explanation layer that is ready to drop in, plus the irregular income detection rules. That is a licence or acqui-hire case, not a moat. What would strengthen it: A named bank sponsor, a pilot letter of intent, or a design that has been through user testing in several languages that the bank has not done.

  5. Question: Where does the data come from? Best honest answer today: Today, a CSV upload parsed in the browser. In production, from the host bank's own transaction feed. Open finance in South Africa is not yet a reliable third party route. What would strengthen it: A one page integration spec and a working import of a real bank's CSV and PDF statement formats.

  6. Question: Who pays, and how much? Best honest answer today: Not decided. The users cannot be the payers. The likely model is a per active user licence to a bank or a funder for financial inclusion. Any price is an assumption. What would strengthen it: A bank value hypothesis with numbers from public sources, for example fee and failed debit order costs for this segment, and a pilot to measure it.

  7. Question: Your Short persona is told R310 is safe to spend today and the chart says Enough. Which number should she believe? Best honest answer today: That is a real inconsistency in the demo. The figures come from three rules: run-out without income, balance spread to the latest next money, and a 30 day path that includes income. It should be fixed so one rule drives the screen. What would strengthen it: Fix it before submission and show the fixed screen in the demo.

  8. Question: Is "safe to spend" or "safe to lend" financial advice, and who is liable if it is wrong? Best honest answer today: It is presented as an estimate, not advice, but the wording is close to advice and has not had legal review. Inside a bank it would sit under the bank's own disclosure and compliance process. What would strengthen it: A legal opinion on the wording, and renamed outputs that describe consequences ("you would stay Tight") rather than permissions ("safe").

  9. Question: Your users run on cash, stokvels and family transfers. How does a bank statement capture that? Best honest answer today: It does not. The engine only sees bank transactions, so cash income and cash spend are invisible. What would strengthen it: A manual cash entry, and data from user sessions on what share of money moves outside the account.

  10. Question: You say 11 official languages. Can I see isiZulu? Best honest answer today: No. The structure for 11 languages exists and every key is in place, but all 10 non-English files are still English. Translation has not been run or reviewed. What would strengthen it: Two languages translated and checked by first-language speakers, and an honest stat on the landing page until the rest are done.

Deal breakers

  • Evidence after testing that users read the date as a payday or a deadline and make worse decisions because of it, or that a Short user acts on "safe to spend".
  • Backtest on real statements showing the actual run-out date falls inside the band much less often than the band implies, with no fix in sight.
  • No bank or funder willing to host it, since there is no viable consumer revenue in this segment and no third party data route.
  • A bank shipping the same view inside its own app before a pilot, which removes the only distribution path.
  • Continued public claims that are not true (11 languages, "machine made") after being flagged.

What would change my mind

  • Recorded sessions with 5 to 8 real irregular earners where most read the date correctly and describe a specific decision they would change.
  • A backtest on real, consented data with error in days and band coverage that is reported honestly, even if modest.
  • A letter of intent or named sponsor at a bank for a small pilot, with an agreed success measure (for example fewer failed debit orders or unpaid fees in the pilot group).
  • The three contradictions on Home fixed and at least two languages properly translated.

Is the core insight defensible

As a technology, no. The date is balance divided by a spend rate, minus known bills, which any bank with transaction history can compute, and FNB already computes the after-debits figure (BusinessTech, 11 June 2018). As a product decision for irregular earners, it is partly defensible: detecting next money without a payday, showing a range, and saying Enough, Tight or Short in plain language in many languages is real design work that a bank may not prioritise for this segment. Defensibility would come from user evidence, tested copy in 11 languages, and a bank relationship, not from code. The likely outcome if the idea proves out is that a bank copies or licenses it. The founder should pitch for that outcome openly.

Score

4 out of 10. A careful, honest prototype of an easily copied feature, with no users, no data route, no buyer and visible contradictions on its key screen.

Sources

  • ITWeb, FNB nav smart budget, 9 November 2020, https://www.itweb.co.za/content/DZQ58MVPaJOvzXy2 (accessed 2026-10-20)

  • Product Hunt, Pocket Runway, "Know what's safe to spend before your next paycheck", https://www.producthunt.com/products/pocket-runway (accessed 2026-10-20)
